Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I’m here because this has been going on for months now, and I can’t keep pretending it’s all just random mistakes. It started with little things, or at least things other people call little—my sister’s appointments getting moved around without anyone really explaining it, bills showing up wrong, people saying they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they “forgot,” or they “didn’t get the message.”

And I know everyone keeps telling me, “Oh, that happens, offices are disorganized,” but it’s the same pattern over and over. It’s not just one mistake. It feels like information is being kept from me, like decisions are being made and then I’m only told after the fact, or not told at all. And I’m being pushed out of things I should absolutely be involved in.

I’m not trying to sound dramatic. I’ve worked in offices—I know the difference between normal clerical errors and when people are steering things a certain way. And lately it’s like every time I try to get a straight answer, there’s another excuse. So I came in because people around me are acting like I’m overreacting, but I know what I’m seeing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d use the word “harm” like someone is going to physically hurt me, but yes, I do think there are people deliberately working around me. Plotting might sound too strong to some people, but that’s what it feels like when the same things keep happening over and over.

For months now, it’s been this pattern—my sister’s appointments get moved and somehow I’m not told until afterward, or I find out by accident. Bills come in wrong, and then when I ask for an explanation, suddenly no one knows who handled it. People say they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they claim they left a message that I never got. And every single time, if I point out that this isn’t normal, I’m treated like I’m making too much of it.

But I know how offices work. I supervised administrative staff for years. Mistakes happen, sure, but not like this, not with the same kind of result every time—me being left out, me not having the information, me being made to look like I’m difficult or uninformed. So yes, I believe information is being kept from me on purpose, and I believe they’re trying to push me out of decisions I have every right to be part of.

It’s not just one mistake. It feels like information is being kept from me, like decisions are being made and then I’m only told after the fact, or not told at all. And I’m being pushed out of things I should absolutely be involved in.\n\nI’m not trying to sound dramatic. I’ve worked in offices—I know the difference between normal clerical errors and when people are steering things a certain way. And lately it’s like every time I try to get a straight answer, there’s another excuse. So I came in because people around me are acting like I’m overreacting, but I know what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is deliberately trying to harm you or plot against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word “harm” like someone is going to physically hurt me, but yes, I do think there are people deliberately working around me. Plotting might sound too strong to some people, but that’s what it feels like when the same things keep happening over and over.\n\nFor months now, it’s been this pattern—my sister’s appointments get moved and somehow I’m not told until afterward, or I find out by accident. Bills come in wrong, and then when I ask for an explanation, suddenly no one knows who handled it. People say they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they claim they left a message that I never got. And every single time, if I point out that this isn’t normal, I’m treated like I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut I know how offices work. I supervised administrative staff for years. Mistakes happen, sure, but not like this, not with the same kind of result every time—me being left out, me not having the information, me being made to look like I’m difficult or uninformed. So yes, I believe information is being kept from me on purpose, and I believe they’re trying to push me out of decisions I have every right to be part of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No, I wouldn’t say I hear voices when no one is there. Not like that. I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. So no, I haven’t had some stretch where I needed much less sleep and felt full of energy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not depressed most of the day nearly every day. I get upset, and I get worn down by things, especially when I feel like I’m not being listened to or people are acting like I’m the problem. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really in the way you’re asking. I mean, I’ve always thought you should be careful with people—at work especially, you learn not everyone says exactly what they mean—but I wouldn’t say my whole life I’ve been unable to trust people’s motives.

This has really been more recent. I’d say it’s been going on about… maybe 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started noticing how much depends on what people tell you, what they leave out, whether they call back when they say they will, whether the information lines up. Before that, I don’t think I was walking around suspicious of everyone. It’s more that once I had to manage all of that, I started paying much closer attention.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because this has been going on for months now, and I can’t keep pretending it’s all just random mistakes. It started with little things, or at least things other people call little—my sister’s appointments getting moved around without anyone really explaining it, bills showing up wrong, people saying they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they “forgot,” or they “didn’t get the message.”\n\nAnd I know everyone keeps telling me, “Oh, that happens, offices are disorganized,” but it’s the same pattern over and over. It’s not just one mistake. It feels like information is being kept from me, like decisions are being made and then I’m only told after the fact, or not told at all. And I’m being pushed out of things I should absolutely be involved in.\n\nI’m not trying to sound dramatic. I’ve worked in offices—I know the difference between normal clerical errors and when people are steering things a certain way. And lately it’s like every time I try to get a straight answer, there’s another excuse. So I came in because people around me are acting like I’m overreacting, but I know what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is deliberately trying to harm you or plot against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word “harm” like someone is going to physically hurt me, but yes, I do think there are people deliberately working around me. Plotting might sound too strong to some people, but that’s what it feels like when the same things keep happening over and over.\n\nFor months now, it’s been this pattern—my sister’s appointments get moved and somehow I’m not told until afterward, or I find out by accident. Bills come in wrong, and then when I ask for an explanation, suddenly no one knows who handled it. People say they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they claim they left a message that I never got. And every single time, if I point out that this isn’t normal, I’m treated like I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut I know how offices work. I supervised administrative staff for years. Mistakes happen, sure, but not like this, not with the same kind of result every time—me being left out, me not having the information, me being made to look like I’m difficult or uninformed. So yes, I believe information is being kept from me on purpose, and I believe they’re trying to push me out of decisions I have every right to be part of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I hear voices when no one is there. Not like that. I mean, sometimes I’ll replay conversations in my head, like anyone does when they’re trying to figure out what someone meant or why they said something a certain way, but that’s my own thinking. I’m not hearing a voice in the room or someone talking to me when nobody’s there.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is more what people do or don’t do—missed calls, things not being passed along, explanations that don’t add up. It’s not voices. It’s the pattern of what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently had a period where you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, my sleep hasn’t been great sometimes, but that’s mostly because I’m lying there thinking through things—like, trying to remember exactly who said what, whether I missed something, whether I should have written down a name or a time. It’s not like I’m sleeping two hours and then waking up feeling wonderful and energized, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIf anything, when I don’t sleep, I feel worn out and irritated because I know I need to be clearheaded to deal with all of this. So no, I haven’t had some stretch where I needed much less sleep and felt full of energy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not depressed most of the day nearly every day. I get upset, and I get worn down by things, especially when I feel like I’m not being listened to or people are acting like I’m the problem. But “depressed” feels like a different word to me.\n\nI still get up and do what I need to do. Some days I’m frustrated, or angry, or tired of having to keep track of everything so carefully. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling sad all day, every day. It’s more that the situation weighs on me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before these recent months, have you generally been someone who has long found it hard to trust other people’s motives?\n\nPatient: No, not really in the way you’re asking. I mean, I’ve always thought you should be careful with people—at work especially, you learn not everyone says exactly what they mean—but I wouldn’t say my whole life I’ve been unable to trust people’s motives.\n\nThis has really been more recent. I’d say it’s been going on about… maybe 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started noticing how much depends on what people tell you, what they leave out, whether they call back when they say they will, whether the information lines up. Before that, I don’t think I was walking around suspicious of everyone. It’s more that once I had to manage all of that, I started paying much closer attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, people have said I’m too focused on what’s going on with my sister’s appointments and trying to get straight answers from people, and maybe they get impatient when I go back over details. But I don’t think anyone has said I’m disorganized or hard to follow in the sense that I’m not making sense.

Outside of all of that—outside the calls, the appointments, the things I’m trying to keep straight—I still keep my home in order. I pay my bills. I go to work and do my job. I’m not falling apart in every area of my life or acting strangely all over the place. It’s more that this one situation takes up a lot of my attention, and people may not like how much I focus on it, but that’s different from being disorganized.

I get upset, and I get worn down by things, especially when I feel like I’m not being listened to or people are acting like I’m the problem. But “depressed” feels like a different word to me.\n\nI still get up and do what I need to do. Some days I’m frustrated, or angry, or tired of having to keep track of everything so carefully. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling sad all day, every day. It’s more that the situation weighs on me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before these recent months, have you generally been someone who has long found it hard to trust other people’s motives?\n\nPatient: No, not really in the way you’re asking. I mean, I’ve always thought you should be careful with people—at work especially, you learn not everyone says exactly what they mean—but I wouldn’t say my whole life I’ve been unable to trust people’s motives.\n\nThis has really been more recent. I’d say it’s been going on about… maybe 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started noticing how much depends on what people tell you, what they leave out, whether they call back when they say they will, whether the information lines up. Before that, I don’t think I was walking around suspicious of everyone. It’s more that once I had to manage all of that, I started paying much closer attention.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, people have said I’m too focused on what’s going on with my sister’s appointments and trying to get straight answers from people, and maybe they get impatient when I go back over details. But I don’t think anyone has said I’m disorganized or hard to follow in the sense that I’m not making sense.\n\nOutside of all of that—outside the calls, the appointments, the things I’m trying to keep straight—I still keep my home in order. I pay my bills. I go to work and do my job. I’m not falling apart in every area of my life or acting strangely all over the place. It’s more that this one situation takes up a lot of my attention, and people may not like how much I focus on it, but that’s different from being disorganized.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because this has been going on for months now, and I can’t keep pretending it’s all just random mistakes. It started with little things, or at least things other people call little—my sister’s appointments getting moved around without anyone really explaining it, bills showing up wrong, people saying they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they “forgot,” or they “didn’t get the message.”\n\nAnd I know everyone keeps telling me, “Oh, that happens, offices are disorganized,” but it’s the same pattern over and over. It’s not just one mistake. It feels like information is being kept from me, like decisions are being made and then I’m only told after the fact, or not told at all. And I’m being pushed out of things I should absolutely be involved in.\n\nI’m not trying to sound dramatic. I’ve worked in offices—I know the difference between normal clerical errors and when people are steering things a certain way. And lately it’s like every time I try to get a straight answer, there’s another excuse. So I came in because people around me are acting like I’m overreacting, but I know what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is deliberately trying to harm you or plot against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word “harm” like someone is going to physically hurt me, but yes, I do think there are people deliberately working around me. Plotting might sound too strong to some people, but that’s what it feels like when the same things keep happening over and over.\n\nFor months now, it’s been this pattern—my sister’s appointments get moved and somehow I’m not told until afterward, or I find out by accident. Bills come in wrong, and then when I ask for an explanation, suddenly no one knows who handled it. People say they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they claim they left a message that I never got. And every single time, if I point out that this isn’t normal, I’m treated like I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut I know how offices work. I supervised administrative staff for years. Mistakes happen, sure, but not like this, not with the same kind of result every time—me being left out, me not having the information, me being made to look like I’m difficult or uninformed. So yes, I believe information is being kept from me on purpose, and I believe they’re trying to push me out of decisions I have every right to be part of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I hear voices when no one is there. Not like that. I mean, sometimes I’ll replay conversations in my head, like anyone does when they’re trying to figure out what someone meant or why they said something a certain way, but that’s my own thinking. I’m not hearing a voice in the room or someone talking to me when nobody’s there.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is more what people do or don’t do—missed calls, things not being passed along, explanations that don’t add up. It’s not voices. It’s the pattern of what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently had a period where you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, my sleep hasn’t been great sometimes, but that’s mostly because I’m lying there thinking through things—like, trying to remember exactly who said what, whether I missed something, whether I should have written down a name or a time. It’s not like I’m sleeping two hours and then waking up feeling wonderful and energized, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIf anything, when I don’t sleep, I feel worn out and irritated because I know I need to be clearheaded to deal with all of this. So no, I haven’t had some stretch where I needed much less sleep and felt full of energy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not depressed most of the day nearly every day. Before that, I don’t think I was walking around suspicious of everyone. It’s more that once I had to manage all of that, I started paying much closer attention.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Mistakes happen, sure, but not like this, not with the same kind of result every time—me being left out, me not having the information, me being made to look like I’m difficult or uninformed. So yes, I believe information is being kept from me on purpose, and I believe they’re trying to push me out of decisions I have every right to be part of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I hear voices when no one is there. It’s more that this one situation takes up a lot of my attention, and people may not like how much I focus on it, but that’s different from being disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, has this belief that people are deliberately keeping information from you been present for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been more than a month. I’d say it’s been about 11 months now, really since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care.\n\nThat was when I started noticing the pattern—trying to get information, making calls, waiting for people to call back, and then things not lining up or feeling like I was only getting part of the story. So yes, this hasn’t just been a few days or a bad week. It’s been going on most of this past year.\n\nDoctor: During these past 11 months, have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, euphoric, or excessively energetic?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe anything that way, no. I mean, there have been times I’ve felt keyed up, but that’s more from being stressed or needing to stay on top of things, not like I was euphoric or suddenly full of energy for no reason.\n\nI haven’t had some stretch where I felt unusually “high” or like I could do anything. If anything, when I’ve had more energy, it’s because there were calls to make or things I felt I had to handle. But that’s different from feeling overly happy or energized in the way you’re asking, I think."
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell… I’m here because this has been going on for months now, and I can’t keep pretending it’s all just random mistakes. It started with little things, or at least things other people call little—my sister’s appointments getting moved around without anyone really explaining it, bills showing up wrong, people saying they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they “forgot,” or they “didn’t get the message.”\n\nAnd I know everyone keeps telling me, “Oh, that happens, offices are disorganized,” but it’s the same pattern over and over. It’s not just one mistake. It feels like information is being kept from me, like decisions are being made and then I’m only told after the fact, or not told at all. And I’m being pushed out of things I should absolutely be involved in.\n\nI’m not trying to sound dramatic. I’ve worked in offices—I know the difference between normal clerical errors and when people are steering things a certain way. And lately it’s like every time I try to get a straight answer, there’s another excuse. Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Bills come in wrong, and then when I ask for an explanation, suddenly no one knows who handled it. People say they’ll call me back and then they don’t, or they claim they left a message that I never got. And every single time, if I point out that this isn’t normal, I’m treated like I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut I know how offices work. I supervised administrative staff for years. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, I wouldn’t say I hear voices when no one is there. Not like that. I mean, sometimes I’ll replay conversations in my head, like anyone does when they’re trying to figure out what someone meant or why they said something a certain way, but that’s my own thinking. I’m not hearing a voice in the room or someone talking to me when nobody’s there.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is more what people do or don’t do—missed calls, things not being passed along, explanations that don’t add up. It’s not voices. It’s the pattern of what’s happening.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
{"confirmed":[],"denied":["S020"],"reasoning":{"S020":"The patient explicitly denies hearing voices when no one is present and clarifies that replaying conversations is their own thinking, not an external voice or hallucination."}}

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"additional_requirement_1\",\"name\":\"Additional Requirement 1\",\"description\":\"Functioning is not markedly impaired apart from the impact of the delusion(s) or its ramifications\",\"reason\":\"The question asks whether speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow; this is best addressed by the profile item indicating that outside the delusional concerns, functioning and behavior are not markedly impaired or strange.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

I pay my bills. I go to work and do my job. I’m not falling apart in every area of my life or acting strangely all over the place. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
